- Target nodes: L and J.
- Neighbors:
  - L connects to E and M.
  - J connects to M, I, P, and D.
- Any path from L to J must leave L via either (L, E) or (L, M).

Check paths via M:
- Direct edge (M, J) gives path L–M–J.
- Even if (M, J) is cut, M can still reach J through M–E–I–J or M–E–I–P–J or M–P–J if there were a direct M–P edge (there isn’t). Actual connections from M are to L, E, and J only. Thus removing (M, J) blocks all routes from L through M to J because M would then have to go M–E–… and those are the same as via E considered next.

Check paths via E:
- E connects to F, L, M, I, and B (via E–B diagonal). From E to J there are routes:
  - E–I–J
  - E–I–P–J
  - E–M–J (already blocked if we remove (M, J))
Therefore, to block all remaining E-based paths, remove (E, I).

Verification:
- After removing edges (M, J) and (E, I):
  - From L via M: L–M cannot reach J because M’s only other neighbor E doesn’t connect to J without (E, I), and there is no M–P edge.
  - From L via E: Paths E–I–J and E–I–P–J are cut at (E, I). E–M–J is cut at (M, J). No other edges from E lead toward J.
- No single-edge removal suffices:
  - If only (M, J) is removed, L–E–I–J still connects.
  - If only (E, I) is removed, L–M–J still connects.

Thus the minimal cut has size 2.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(M, J), (E, I)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:

List all E–I paths by following only continuous lines:
- Vertical: E–C–I (since E connects straight down to C, and C to I).
- Through B side: E–B–I (there is a vertical from E to B via the rectangular route E–B, then B goes right to H and around bottom to I? Check carefully: B connects directly down-left to C and right to H; H connects down along outer frame to the bottom edge then left to I, so E–B–H–(outer bottom)–I is a valid path. Also E–B–C–I is valid.)
- Through N/Y/O/P side: E–N–C–I and E–N–B–…; also E–Y–O–P–I via the inner box connections (E connects down to N, left to Y via N–Y, then Y–O–P–I).
- Through left outer frame: E–M–J–(bottom-left frame)–I is also connected because E–M horizontal, M down to J via left border, then bottom border to I.
- Through right side: E–F–K–G–H–(outer bottom)–I is another route via the right frame.

Observation: Every route from E to I must pass through one of two “gateway” nodes immediately below E: either C or N (from E straight down). However, E also has horizontal connections: to M (left) and to F (right). Those give routes that bypass C/N unless we also cut at the outer frame. But check: From E to left M, to go to I you must eventually enter the bottom/center area. The only entrances from the left or top into the bottom center connect through J/P chain or through C/N/B/H chain, which ultimately still funnels through either C or P/H toward I. Specifically:
- Left route E–M–J–P–I requires P.
- Right route E–F–(X/K/G/H)–… to reach I uses H on the right frame, then bottom edge to I; that relies on H.

Thus, to disconnect E from I, it suffices to cut the three “funnels” into I: C, P, and H. Verify:
- Remove C: breaks direct vertical E–C–I and any path using C (E–N–C–I, E–B–C–I).
- Remove P: breaks left/inner-bottom access J–P–I and O–P–I and Y–O–P–I.
- Remove H: breaks the entire right-frame route E–F–…–H–(bottom)–I and also E–B–H–(bottom)–I.

After removing {C, P, H}, is there any remaining E–I path? The bottom outer edge from right to I is severed at H; the left-bottom to I is severed at P; the central vertical is severed at C. No alternate edge touches I except from C (above) and P (left) and the bottom-right coming from H; there are no other nodes adjacent to I. Therefore all paths are blocked.

Can we do it with fewer than 3 nodes?
- With only two nodes removed:
  - If we remove C and P, a path remains via the right: E–F–K–G–H–(bottom)–I.
  - If we remove C and H, a path remains via the left: E–M–J–P–I.
  - If we remove P and H, a path remains via the center: E–C–I (or E–N–C–I).
Hence any 2-node set fails.

Therefore the minimum cut is 3 nodes, for example {C, P, H}.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [C, P, H]

- Besides (B, H), check for alternative routes:
  1) B connects upward to E via vertical edge (B, E). From E, there is a horizontal edge E–F, and F connects down to X, which connects right to K, which connects down to G, which connects down to H. So path: B–E–F–X–K–G–H.

  4) From B up to E, then up to L, left to M, down the left border to J, right to I, then to H: B–E–L–M–J–I–H. Similar variants exist via J–P–I–H.

Thus multiple disjoint-looking branches carry flow from B’s neighborhood to H’s neighborhood. To disconnect, cut a minimum set that hits all B-to-H paths.

Observe the bottlenecks near H:
- All alternative paths (that avoid the direct edge B–H) ultimately must enter H through either edge (G, H) from above-right or through the bottom edge (I, H) from the bottom, or through the direct edge (B, H).
- There is no other edge incident to H.

Therefore, removing the three edges incident to H, namely (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H), will certainly disconnect B from H.
- Necessity/minimality: If we remove fewer than 3 edges, at least one of the three edges to H remains. 
  - If (B, H) remains, B is directly connected to H.
  - If (I, H) remains, there are paths B–N–C–I–H or B–E–L–M–J–I–H, etc.
  - If (G, H) remains, there is a path B–E–F–X–K–G–H.
Hence any cut must include all three edges incident to H; thus 3 is minimal.

- List key connections from H toward O:
  - H connects directly to B, I, and G.
  - From B, there are edges to N and C; from N there is a direct long edge to O; from C there is also a direct long edge to O. Therefore any path via B reaches O through either N→O or C→O.
  - From I, there are edges to J, C, and P; J connects to D and P; P connects to O; C connects to O. Thus any path via I reaches O through either C→O or P→O.
  - From G, there are edges to K and F; F connects to M and X; K connects to X; X connects to K and F; M connects down the right ridge to J (then to P→O). Also G connects back to H, so the G-branch ultimately reaches O through J→P→O or via I/C routes.

- Observe the rightmost “gate” to O: all routes from H eventually must pass through either C, P, or N to reach O (since the only nodes adjacent to O are Y, N, C, and P; H has no route to Y except through N/B chain which already uses N).
  - Paths using B lead to O through N or C.
  - Paths using I lead to O through C or P.
  - Paths using G/top cluster lead to J then P to O (or down to I then C/P).

- Therefore removing nodes {C, P, N} would certainly disconnect H from O, but we seek the minimum.
  - If we remove just C and P: any path via B could still use N→O, so H→B→N→O remains.
  - If we remove just N and C: paths via I can use P→O, and via G→…→J→P→O remain.
  - If we remove just N and P: paths via I can use C→O, and via B can use C→O.

- Hence we need to remove at least two nodes, and they must block both “channels”: {N} for the B-branch and one of {C, P} for the I/G branches, or alternatively {C, P} together while also blocking B→N, but that’s three. The minimal choice is two nodes: remove N and either C or P.
  - Verify:
    - Remove N and C: 
      - B-branch: B→N→O is cut (N removed); B→C→O is cut (C removed).
      - I-branch: I→C→O is cut (C removed); I→P→O still exists, but from H to P requires H→I→P or H→G→…→J→P, both of which reach P; however with N present removed, this doesn’t matter unless P leads to O. But P→O remains; is there a path H→…→P? Yes via H→I→P. Thus N and C is not sufficient; O is still reachable via H→I→P→O. So N+C fails.
    - Remove N and P:
      - B-branch: B→N→O cut (N removed); B→C→O still open, so H→B→C→O exists. So N+P fails.
    - Remove N and P is bad; remove N and C we saw fails; therefore the correct pair must be C and P together, plus handling N. But we cannot remove three.

Re-evaluate adjacency to O: The image shows O connected to Y (baseline), N (diagonal), C (long line), and P (right slanted). Are there any paths from H to Y that bypass N? H→B→… to Y via N then Y; no other line from left to Y. So to reach O through Y, one would need N. Thus blocking N alone removes the Y and N ports to O, leaving only C and P.

Now check if both C and P are unavoidable from H when N is removed:
- With N removed, routes to O must go through C or P.
- Can we block both with a single node upstream? Is there a single articulation node that all paths from H to {C, P} must pass through? From H:
  - H→I→C or H→I→P (so I is a common node for reaching both C and P via that branch).
  - H→G→…→J→P (reaches P without I or C), and J also connects to I; but G-branch to C might go G→…→I→C; however to reach P via the G-top cluster you can go G→K→X→F→M→(down right edge) to J and then P without touching I or C. Therefore I is NOT a single choke point.
  - H→B→C possible without passing I/J; H→B does not reach P except maybe B→C→P? There is no C–P edge. From B to P there is a direct line? The picture shows B connected to P by a long straight line? The drawn long line from B goes to O and to C; I do not see a B–P edge. So B only leads to C (and N). Thus if N is removed and we also remove C, the B-branch dies. But the I/G→J→P route still survives.

N removed but B→C→O remains. So fail. Remove M or K etc will not block I-branch and B→C.

Thus all analysis shows that to cut every port to O we must remove all three of {N, C, P}. Since we’re required to find the minimum, and no 2-node set suffices, the minimum is 3.

Final Answer:

No other connections exist from O to H.

- Block both remaining branches’ access to N by cutting bottlenecks closer to N:
   - From I-branch, there are two disjoint ways to N: via C and via O. Cutting (H,C) already blocks the via-C part; we must block via-O: cut (O,N) or (I,P) or (P,O). But G-branch can also reach O, so the chosen edge should stop both I- and G-branches. Cutting (O,N) is good because both branches eventually need O→N or B→N or C→N.
   - But G-branch also has G–B–N and G–C–N routes. We already cut (H,C), but G can go to C without using H? From H we go to G first, so path is H–G–C–N; (H,C) removal doesn’t affect G–C; that path uses C–N which is still intact. So we must also cut (C,N) or (G,C). Cutting (C,N) would block both H–G–C–N and H–I–C–N.
So choose edges: (H,C), (C,N), (O,N). Now check:
- Paths using B–N: From H via G to B to N: H–G–B–N would still exist. So we must also address B–N. That would be a fourth edge.
Maybe instead of cutting at N twice, find a single upstream edge that blocks both B and O routes? They converge at P? No, B route is H–G–B–N; it doesn’t pass through P/O. There’s no single edge that simultaneously blocks the G→B shortcut and the I→P→O path except removing (H,G) which kills G-branch entirely. Let’s try:
Pick (H,G) to eliminate all G-based routes (including via B and via C and via O through upper area). Then we just need to block I-branch.
- Remove (H,G).
- For I-branch, block both subroutes to N: via C and via O. We can cut (C,N) and (O,N) at N, or instead cut a single edge earlier that is common to both subroutes. Is there a single edge common to both I→C→N and I→P→O→N? From I outward, the only common edge is (H,I) or starting from I itself: both paths start with I. The two subroutes share edge (I, something)? One is I–C, the other is I–P; they are disjoint. Thus need two cuts unless we isolate N by removing (Y,N) and one of (C,N)/(O,N)/(B,N) while also making Y unreachable. But with (H,G) removed, can H still reach Y? Yes via H–I–P–O–Y. So to stop I-branch with two edges, cutting (C,N) and (O,N) suffices, regardless of Y since Y connects to O and N; even if H reaches Y, without (Y,N) cut, Y–N is still available giving path H–…–Y–N. But to reach Y you use O–Y; with (O,N) removed, Y–N still exists; H can reach Y via O; then Y–N uses untouched edge; hence we must also cut (Y,N) OR break reaching Y by cutting (O,Y). That adds another edge.
Therefore better to cut (H,I) instead of cutting at N:
Try set: (H,G), (H,I), (H,C) — that’s 3 edges and clearly disconnects H from graph, minimal 3.

Can we do with 2 edges? No, because H has three edge-disjoint immediate neighbors; with only two cuts, one neighbor remains connected, and there is a path from that neighbor to N:
- If (H,C) remains, path H–C–N exists.
- If (H,I) remains, path H–I–C–N or H–I–P–O–N exists.
- If (H,G) remains, path H–G–B–N exists.
Thus at least 3 edges are required.

Therefore minimal cut is removing all three edges incident to H.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, G), (H, I), (H, C)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [['F', 'E'], ['H', 'B'], ['H', 'I']]}
